=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a string s and an integer k.
In one operation, you can replace the character at any position with the next or previous letter in the alphabet (wrapping around so that 'a' is after 'z'). For example, replacing 'a' with the next letter results in 'b', and replacing 'a' with the previous letter results in 'z'. Similarly, replacing 'z' with the next letter results in 'a', and replacing 'z' with the previous letter results in 'y'.
Return the length of the longest palindromic subsequence of s that can be obtained after performing at most k operations.
 
Example 1:

Input: s = "abced", k = 2
Output: 3
Explanation:

Replace s[1] with the next letter, and s becomes "acced".
Replace s[4] with the previous letter, and s becomes "accec".

The subsequence "ccc" forms a palindrome of length 3, which is the maximum.

Example 2:

Input: s = "aaazzz", k = 4
Output: 6
Explanation:

Replace s[0] with the previous letter, and s becomes "zaazzz".
Replace s[4] with the next letter, and s becomes "zaazaz".
Replace s[3] with the next letter, and s becomes "zaaaaz".

The entire string forms a palindrome of length 6.

 
Constraints:

1 <= s.length <= 200
1 <= k <= 200
s consists of only lowercase English letters.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def longestPalindromicSubsequence(self, s: str, k: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

        K=k
        # dp0 for length 0: list of [0]*(K+1) for starts 0..n maybe

            for l in range(n-length+1):

                    best = skip_l[b]
                    if skip_r[b] > best: best = skip_r[b]

Let's check code syntax:

class Solution:
    def longestPalindromicSubsequence(self, s: str, k: int) -> int:
        n = len(s)
        K = k
        if n == 1:
            return 1

        dp0 = [[0] * (K + 1) for _ in range(n + 1)]
        dp1 = [[1] * (K + 1) for _ in range(n)]

        prev2 = dp0
        prev1 = dp1

        for length in range(2, n + 1):
            curr = [[0] * (K + 1) for _ in range(n - length + 1)]
            for l in range(n - length + 1):
                r = l + length - 1
                a = ord(s[l]) - 97
                b = ord(s[r]) - 97
                diff = abs(a - b)
                if diff > 13:
                    diff = 26 - diff
                cost = diff

                skip_l = prev1[l + 1]
                skip_r = prev1[l]
                match = prev2[l + 1]

                row = curr[l]
                # maybe loop b
                for ops in range(K + 1):
                    best = skip_l[ops]
                    if skip_r[ops] > best:
                        best = skip_r[ops]
                    if cost <= ops:
                        val = 2 + match[ops - cost]
                        if val > best:
                            best = val
                    row[ops] = best
            prev2, prev1 = prev1, curr

        return prev1[0][K]

Let's write final code cleanly:

class Solution:
    def longestPalindromicSubsequence(self, s: str, k: int) -> int:
        n = len(s)
        if n == 1:
            return 1

        K = k
        # dp0: intervals of length 0
        dp0 = [[0] * (K + 1) for _ in range(n + 1)]
        # dp1: intervals of length 1
        dp1 = [[1] * (K + 1) for _ in range(n)]

        prev2 = dp0  # length - 2
        prev1 = dp1  # length - 1

        for length in range(2, n + 1):
            curr = [[0] * (K + 1) for _ in range(n - length + 1)]
            for l in range(n - length + 1):
                r = l + length - 1
                x = ord(s[l]) - 97
                y = ord(s[r]) - 97
                diff = abs(x - y)
                if diff > 13:
                    diff = 26 - diff
                cost = diff

                skip_l = prev1[l + 1]
                skip_r = prev1[l]
                match = prev2[l + 1]
                row = curr[l]

                for ops in range(K + 1):
                    best = skip_l[ops]
                    if skip_r[ops] > best:
                        best = skip_r[ops]
                    if cost <= ops:
                        cand = 2 + match[ops - cost]
                        if cand > best:
                            best = cand
                    row[ops] = best

            prev2, prev1 = prev1, curr

        return prev1[0][K]

Now, let's think about if the DP recurrence with pair cost dist(s[l],s[r]) and inner dp[l+1][r-1] handles the case where l and r are not paired in optimal but one of them is paired with an inner char and the other skipped. Standard LPS recurrence: dp[l][r] = max(dp[l+1][r], dp[l][r-1], 2+dp[l+1][r-1] if match). Does this always cover cases where l is paired with some j<r and r skipped? dp[l][r-1] includes l paired with j within [l,r-1]. Cases where r paired with some i>l and l skipped: dp[l+1][r]. Cases where both l and r are paired with inner chars but not each other? If l paired with j<r and r paired with i>l, with i<j? Actually in a palindromic subsequence, pairs are nested. If l is paired with some j, then the counterpart of r (if r is in subsequence) must be some i with l < i < j? Wait let's reason. Standard LPS recurrence is correct for subsequences: For interval [l,r], any palindromic subsequence either excludes l, excludes r, or includes both l and r as a pair. If it includes l but not r, then it's in [l,r-1]. If includes r but not l, in [l+1,r]. If includes both, they must be paired together because l is leftmost and r is rightmost; in a palindrome, the leftmost selected character pairs with the rightmost selected character. If both l and r are selected, they are the outermost pair, so they pair with each other. Thus recurrence covers. Good.
